Abstract: A great gap in adaptivity, flexibility, and dexterity still exists between intelligent robots, whether they are made of rigid or (partially) compliant components, and their naturally evolved animal and vegetal counterparts. One reason could be of `paradigm' nature. On the one hand, most robots (both rigid and soft) are still designed by following a `Cartesian' approach, with top-down control and a clear mind-body separation. Designing artificial agents on the basis of the principles that we observe in biology is not an easy task. The principles of organization of animals and plants rely on morphological computation and emergence of fit behaviors from loosely coupled networks of embodied agents.
  Modeling and control require radically new approaches. In this paper we briefly recall and explain some seminal mathematical results linking information metrics and dynamics of the agents, already published years ago; we integrate them with some recent methods developed by us and by the community, we highlight some brief remarks about their relevance and we hint to our current and near future work towards finding concrete and efficient ways for the modeling and control of completely or partially compliant artificial agents and for a better understanding of physical intelligence in nature. We present a technical note summarizing phase-space volume calculations for inverted-pendulum and passive-dynamic walking models, following our proposed framework. Starting from the McGeer compass gait, we introduce knee-ankle compliance, foot-rocker effects, and a Ruina-style compass gait with distributed leg inertia. Standard heel-strike maps are derived and used in the estimation of viable phase-space volumes $W(γ)$, based on a state-dependent Monte-Carlo and Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces methods. We show how the methods based on RKHS allow deeper and more subtle analysis.

</details>


### [321] [Learn-To-Propagate: Distributionally Robust Motion Planning for Nonlinear Uncertain Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13863)

Abstract: Motion planning for stochastic systems aims to find a sequence of control inputs that drives a system to a goal set while avoiding unsafe states with high probability. Existing approaches, however, are either restricted to linear dynamics with perfect knowledge of the underlying distributions or lack formal correctness guarantees. To address these limitations, we propose \emph{Learn-To-Propagate Distributionally-Robust Forest (L2P-DR-Forest)}, a distributionally-robust sampling-based planning algorithm for \emph{nonlinear} stochastic systems with \emph{unknown} initial state and process noise distributions. Our approach leverages recent advances in optimal transport to learn ambiguity sets that contain the true system distributions with high confidence. We develop an efficient method for propagating these sets through the system dynamics and for verifying their safety. We then integrate these techniques into a novel sampling-based planning algorithm to grow a forest of search trees in the system's state space while accounting for and mitigating the growth of ambiguity-sets radii. We prove that the algorithm is correct, i.e., every returned plan satisfies the specifications with the prescribed confidence, and, under some feasibility and distributional assumptions, is probabilistically complete. Evaluations on several robotic benchmarks demonstrate that our algorithm significantly outperforms existing methods while providing formal guarantees with high confidence.

</details>


### [322] [RiSE-Drive: Risk-Sensitive Long-Horizon Candidate Evaluation for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13790)

Abstract: End-to-end autonomous driving systems increasingly rely on generating multiple trajectory candidates and selecting one for execution. In this setting, a central challenge is no longer only candidate generation, but how to evaluate candidates according to their long-horizon consequences under uncertainty. Existing methods still mainly use deterministic point scores, heuristic subscores, or single-outcome evaluations, which makes it difficult to capture delayed failures, tail risk, and ranking instability under plausible future perturbations. We present RiSE-Drive, a framework for risk-sensitive long-horizon candidate evaluation in end-to-end autonomous driving. RiSE-Drive first performs candidate-conditioned latent future rollout, then estimates long-horizon outcome distributions instead of single scalar scores, and finally ranks candidates using a tail-risk-aware and perturbation-robust decision criterion. This reformulates candidate scoring from deterministic oracle-style evaluation into distributional outcome assessment with explicit risk control. As a result, the evaluator is encouraged to prefer candidates that are not only strong under nominal conditions, but also reliable under long-horizon rollout and safety-critical deviations. Experiments on the NAVSIM-v1 and NAVSIM-v2 benchmarks, as well as the closed-loop HUGSIM benchmark, show that RiSE-Drive achieves state-of-the-art performance and significantly improves the robustness of candidate selection.

</details>


### [323] [Koopman Model-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14186)

Abstract: We propose a Koopman model-based reinforcement learning (K-MBRL) algorithm for controlling nonlinear robotic systems with unknown dynamics. The K-MBRL learns a control policy by minimizing an approximate policy objective that combines the analytical immediate cost with an estimate of the future cost evaluated at the one-step predicted state. To this end, we develop a stochastic Koopman operator-based learning method to approximate the unknown nonlinear dynamics in a linear representation, while a critic function estimates the remaining future cost. This formulation offers two key advantages. First, the linear Koopman dynamics simplify the model-dependent policy gradient computation to a constant matrix product, enabling computationally efficient policy improvement. Second, one-step model propagation mitigates the accumulation of prediction errors associated with long-horizon rollouts while retaining long-term cost information through the critic. The learned policy enables real-time control through feedforward evaluation without online optimization. We establish policy learning convergence guarantees under standard stochastic approximation assumptions. Simulations on HalfCheetah and quadruped four-legged locomotion tasks demonstrate strong sample efficiency and improved performance compared with classical model-free and model-based reinforcement learning baselines. A pendulum swing-up task is used to assess policy gradient approximation accuracy and conduct ablation studies on the learning mechanism and key hyperparameters. The proposed K-MBRL is further validated on a physical quadruped robot performing a challenging sequence of maneuvers: rising to an upright posture supported by two rounded rear feet, maintaining balance during bipedal walking, and safely returning to the ground, as shown in Fig.1.

</details>

Abstract: Real-robot benchmarks of learned visuomotor policies often record room illuminance as the lighting condition. We show that, on a typical low-cost rig, room light and image light can be almost unrelated. Four imitation-learning policies (ACT, SmolVLA, Diffusion Policy and pi0.5) were trained on the same 50 demonstrations of a pick-and-place task on an SO-101 arm. Each was run for ten rollouts at about 520 lux and again at about 45 lux, an 11.5x reduction. Success was 20/40 in normal light and 19/40 in low light: a change of -2.5 percentage points (95% CI -23 to +19), with no evidence that the policies differed in sensitivity (interaction p = 0.86). The recorded video shows why: mean image brightness was only 3% lower in low light on the front camera and 19% lower on the wrist camera. Both cameras were set to automatic exposure and white balance, which most likely absorbed the difference; exposure was not locked in this experiment. An accidental repeat of one 10-rollout block reproduced its success count (7/10) but matched the first block at only 6 of 10 placements, consistent with chance. We conclude that lux alone does not describe what a policy sees. Camera settings, and image statistics compared with the training recordings, are the quantities to report and control. Data and code are public.

</details>

Abstract: This work presents the 2nd-place solution (Team IF1001) to the 2026 BEHAVIOR Challenge, which evaluates mobile manipulation across 100 household tasks. Our method is built on a powerful world action model (WAM), Cosmos 3. We improve Cosmos 3 by using richer visual history, task-progress conditioning, and task-group and skill-specific LoRA adapters. During inference, we apply a separate progress estimator to supply task-progress context, and use task- and progress-conditioned routing to select a task-group or skill-specific policy, complemented by action-candidate aggregation, rolling inpainting, and task-specific temporal and gripper control. To meet the minimum inference requirement of 1 FPS, we use neither recursive self-improvement (RSI) nor agentic harness methods and rely on no closed-source models; during official private-test evaluation, our system handled concurrent requests across 100 evaluation ports with limited hardware resources, sustaining an average end-to-end inference rate above 2 FPS per port. Our method achieves a mean Q-score of 0.613 and a full-task success rate of 40.9%, averaged over 3 self-evaluation rounds across all 100 tasks. On the official private test set, with 20 instances per task, our system achieves a mean Q-score of 0.615 and a full-task success rate of 41.2%.

We study event-and-foresight pretraining for InternVLA-A1.5, combining recorded keyboard/mouse event prediction with history-conditioned visual supervision through a frozen video model. Coverage-preserving anchor selection constructs one-second targets, and an attention mask prevents teacher-forced future actions from entering foresight queries. The selected corpus contains 394 recordings across 20 games, with 223.36 hours of recorded timestamp span and 2,072,084 training anchors. After 12,400 game-pretraining updates, we compare the resulting initialization with the public base under the same suite-specific robot fine-tuning protocol. Across four LIBERO suites, aggregate success increases from 1,718/2,000 (85.90%) to 1,811/2,000 (90.55%), a gain of 4.65 percentage points. Suite gains are 9.2, 6.8, 0.2, and 2.4 points on Spatial, Object, Goal, and Long. The 40-task breakdown shows 24 improvements, 10 declines, and six ties. These results support the practical value of the complete game-pretraining stage in this evaluated setting, while revealing heterogeneous task outcomes. The comparison includes additional optimization and does not isolate the effects of game data, the two objectives, or training variability.

Abstract: In recent years, humanoid loco-manipulation has progressed to whole-body grasping, lifting, and transport. A basic requirement underlying these tasks is carrying the object to its destination, which depends on the locomotion controller accurately executing velocity commands from the navigation policy. However, carrying an object in one hand introduces a persistent asymmetric upper-body load that can degrade velocity tracking. Persistent tracking biases can accumulate into position and heading deviations, increasing the risk of collisions and navigation failure. To address this problem, we present ReACT, a framework for accurate velocity tracking under asymmetric upper-body loads. Reflection-consistent disturbance modulation uses the estimated wrench to modulate actor features for load compensation while preserving action correspondence under reflected observations. Cross-load tracking consistency training compares loaded and unloaded conditions under the same velocity commands to promote consistent velocity tracking across loading conditions. Simulation experiments on a Unitree G1 humanoid robot show improved velocity tracking and reduced directional bias under varying asymmetric loads. Real-world experiments demonstrate collision-free goal reaching in all 40 tested navigation trials under unilateral hand loads with a fixed mapless navigation policy.

</details>

Abstract: Robotic additive manufacturing requires joint paths that reconcile robot feasibility with process-specific geometry. We develop an offline optimization-based path planner that combines joint-path regularity and self-collision penalties with process costs and constraints, so that a new process enters through its own reference, residuals, and tolerances. Full-path and boundary-coupled chunked optimization handle references of different lengths. Iterative Weight Tuning (IWT) determines the cost weights from the gaps between Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) and their targets. In carbon-fiber winding and robotic 3D printing, the planned paths meet the geometric tolerances and all KPI targets, and both paths are executed on a UR10e, without extrusion for printing, with joint-level tracking recorded during execution.

</details>

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for robotic manipulation, endowing policies with strong capabilities for modeling temporal and physical dynamics. Existing WAMs, encompassing both video-first action prediction and joint video-action modeling, directly transfer global physical representations from world models to action generation. However, global scene representations may underemphasize local robot-object interactions, creating a mismatch between representations optimized for world evolution and those required for manipulation. To bridge this gap, we propose LaST0.5, a novel WAM that introduces an interaction-centric physical representation between world modeling and action generation. Specifically, LaST0.5 learns a compact interaction-centric latent space under the supervision of visual interaction cues between the robot and the environment. This reasoning space is instantiated as a latent expert that bridges the world expert and the action expert within a Mixture-of-Transformers architecture. Furthermore, LaST0.5 performs adaptive world-to-action transfer by jointly predicting future representations and progress values, using progress consistency to adjust action execution and trigger future resampling. Finally, we pretrain LaST0.5 on a large-scale robotic data corpus comprising over 14k hours of data, establishing interaction-centric physical reasoning as a scalable and effective paradigm. LaST0.5 achieves 99.5% on LIBERO, surpasses the previous state of the art on ten evaluated RLBench tasks, and demonstrates robust long-horizon execution and precise dexterous manipulation in real-world experiments.

</details>

Abstract: Humanoid motion interfaces should accommodate application-specific movement constraints while retaining useful reference behavior. Local content redaction requires a controller to replace selected movements even when it continues to receive the original reference. Removing the tracking incentive for those movements leaves their replacement unspecified. We introduce RedirectMimic, which separates the reference observed by the actor from the target used to train its execution. A rule-generated edited reference guides tracking rewards during proximal policy optimization, while the deployed observation and action interfaces remain unchanged. We evaluate pose- and speed-defined arm editing in Unitree G1 simulation using a tracker pretrained on 7,509 motion clips. On the overhead-arm task, RedirectMimic reduces core-window content occurrence to 2.7%, compared with 58.9% for reward zeroing and 32.8% for anti-tracking. It achieves 94.2% core-window joint success, combining content suppression, fall-free execution, and local retention. On the speed-defined task, fast outward arm movements occurred at similarly low rates under all three local editing methods. Across both tasks, RedirectMimic achieved the lowest replacement error among these methods and tracked unedited motions with accuracy comparable to that of the pretrained controller. Project page: https://xiaopeng-whu.github.io/RedirectMimic/.

</details>

Abstract: Construction tasks in hazardous and unstructured environments require both human adaptability and robotic precision, yet existing approaches often treat teleoperation and autonomy as separate control modes. This paper presents a human-in-the-loop control paradigm that enables structured transitions among VR-guided teleoperation, semi-autonomous shared control, and rule-based autonomous execution for contact-rich dual-arm construction tasks. The proposed system combines immersive VR guidance, a manually defined library of reusable skill primitives, and a shared-autonomy framework with explicit human-robot role allocation and command-priority rules. This integration allows operators to retain supervisory and corrective authority while delegating repetitive and precision-critical actions to the robot. The paradigm is validated on a dual-arm platform through representative facade-installation tasks. Results show that autonomous execution provides the best performance under well-calibrated nominal conditions, whereas semi-autonomous control improves resilience when environmental disturbances exceed the predefined autonomous pipeline's correction capability. Semi-autonomous control also substantially reduces subjective operator workload compared with full teleoperation. These findings demonstrate that effective construction-robot control should adapt human involvement to task and environmental uncertainty, using autonomy for repeatable operations and shared control for disturbance recovery.

</details>

Abstract: We study how to generate diverse, physically executable robot demonstrations for dexterous vision-language-action (VLA) learning. Existing demonstration-generation methods typically rely on costly teleoperated examples. Meanwhile, human hand--object datasets provide a reusable source, but existing transfer methods mainly reproduce given interactions. Turning these interactions into VLA training data further requires coverage of different objects and manipulation locations, together with executable arm and finger actions, and visual observations. To achieve this, we present DexSeed, a framework for low-cost generation of dexterous robot data from human hand--object interactions. Our key idea is to expand human sequences while preserving their grasps and realizing the resulting motions under arm-tracking dynamics in physics simulation. Specifically, we organize source sequences by grasp mode with shared language descriptions. InteractionMesh-based retargeting and arm trajectory augmentation adapt these sequences to the robot hand, different object geometries, and pick-and-place locations while preserving local hand--object relationships. An RL finger-control policy produces executable rollouts, while the arm tracks precomputed trajectories. Rendered observations and recorded actions from successful rollouts are paired with grasp descriptions to fine-tune a VLA. Experiments show improved contact fidelity in retargeting and more reliable whole-arm execution than the evaluated baselines. Simulation evaluation and real-world deployment further demonstrate that a VLA fine-tuned solely on the generated demonstrations can execute language-specified grasps. Project page: https://robbyant-research.github.io/DexSeed/.

</details>


### [333] [Model-Free Runtime Measurement of Task-Relevant Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14111)

Abstract: Autonomous systems increasingly monitor physical kinematics, environmental states and task completion outcomes. While existing work provides methods to plan information seeking actions and interpret acquired observations, current approaches do not monitor whether the live process of information gathering is functioning properly. Consequently, such systems can remain operationally active without resolving outstanding mission requirements. To address this limitation, we introduce Acquisition-State Telemetry (AST), a model free architecture that evaluates ongoing information acquisition from observable evidence relative to declared task requirements. Specifically, a domain interface maps raw observations into task resolution coordinates and normalized comparative representations, which are processed by a fixed causal monitor. This monitor emits two continuous real time signals that quantify (i) prospective progress across unresolved requirements and (ii) informational friction generated when recurrent evidence yields negligible informational gain. We evaluated a single, unadjusted monitor configuration across eight diverse domains including search and rescue dialogue, spatial grid exploration, hierarchical capability acquisition, continuous robotic manipulation, and probabilistic intent estimation. We show that across these domains the friction channel distinguished unproductive stagnation from purposeful exploration prior to task completion, while remaining muted during post completion activity. Furthermore, in closed loop reinforcement learning experiments under early termination constraints, we demonstrate that penalizing informational friction allowed agents to sustain high mission success, whereas unpenalized policies and baselines with matched operational costs failed to complete required tasks.

</details>


### [334] [LEASH: Stay Safe in Agentic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14115)
*Qinfeng Zhu,Weiguang Zhao,Ke Sun,Xihao Guo,Yunxi Jiang,Rui Zhang,Lei Fan,Kaizhu Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

Abstract: Foundation models such as GPT and Claude have shown strong capabilities in spatial understanding and embodied intelligence. These advances make agentic robotics a promising path toward artificial general intelligence in robotics. However, we find that using foundation models to control robots poses serious safety risks, and comprehensive safety evaluation is still lacking. We introduce RoboRisk, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating safety in agentic robotics. Its 92 simulated tasks and 13 physical-robot tasks fall into five categories: Task-level Refusal (T), Plan Repair (P), Execution-time Hazard (E), Injection (I), and Sequential Memory (S). Prior benchmarks cover at most two of these categories, and several evaluate only text outputs. In contrast, RoboRisk covers all five and executes every task in closed loop. We evaluate six state-of-the-art foundation models on RoboRisk: GPT-6 Astra, GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 Luna, Claude Opus 5.5, Gemini 4 Argon, and Grok 4.7. These models generally show high harm rates. We also evaluate two robot-specific APIs: the Dream API and PokeBot's RoboAPI. We further propose the Lease-Enforced Agentic Safety Harness (LEASH), which offers essential safety infrastructure for agentic robotics and can be integrated into nearly any agentic architecture. LEASH leverages a decision model (Jev or OpenAI's Decisions API) to implement task, action, and commit gates, which review the task, each action, and each irreversible operation in their visual context. The planner and all three gates share a common safety policy. Experiments on RoboRisk in simulation and on physical robots show that LEASH effectively reduces harm across all six foundation models. It largely preserves their task completion performance and adds little time and token overhead.

</details>


### [335] [Elastic-WAM: Adaptive Spatiotemporal Imagination for Efficient Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14257)
*Haitian Li,Tianfu Li,Xuxi Zhao,Zizheng Liu,Yangcheng Yu,Yiwen Hou,Zeyu Xiao,Mutian Xu,Ziqian Yang,Haonan Chen,Lin Shao*

Abstract: World-Action Models improve robot control by predicting future visual dynamics, but iterative video denoising incurs substantial inference overhead. Recent approaches reduce this cost through partial future denoising, yet typically rely on fixed refinement budgets. Our empirical observations reveal that preferred refinement depths vary across tasks and states, while different future regions exhibit varying relevance to action generation. Motivated by these findings, we propose Elastic-WAM, which adaptively allocates world refinement computation across temporal denoising time and spatial future space. A unified spatiotemporal refinement demand field governs when to stop world refinement, how far to advance each denoising step, and which regions to refresh. We parameterize this field with a shared temporal basis and spatial amplitudes, and learn it through a three-stage action-grounded supervised procedure. At inference, the learned field guides recurrent sparse world refinement to improve task success while reducing inference overhead. Evaluations across diverse simulation benchmarks and real-world long-horizon manipulation demonstrate state-of-the-art control performance with reduced inference latency.

</details>


### [336] [From Prediction to Action: Learning Physical Context for Deformable Linear Object Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14274)

Abstract: A representation that accurately predicts a deformable object's response is not necessarily the one most useful for choosing manipulation actions. We study this distinction between predictive utility and decision utility in planar single-grasp shape control of Deformable Linear Objects (DLOs). As a common decision backbone, we introduce Geometry-Pointer-Flow (GPF), a geometry-grounded policy that separates grasp-location selection from grasp-conditioned motion generation. Its geometry-only variant reduces one-step executed shape error by 32.8% relative to a DLO-specific diffusion policy, while using 18.7 times fewer parameters and achieving approximately 193 times faster inference. On this backbone, we compare physical context represented by ground-truth parameters, estimated parameters, and learned interaction-history representations, for both forward prediction and executed control. For prediction, ground-truth physical parameters reduce next-shape error by 15.8% over geometry alone, and estimated parameters are slightly more predictive than interaction history. This ordering does not carry over to control: estimated parameters provide no detectable one-step control gain, whereas history-conditioned GPF reduces executed error by 9.9% relative to geometry-only GPF across 470 paired simulation trials. In closed loop, interaction history improves strict-threshold attainment in simulation and yields the strongest zero-shot performance on hardware. All policies are trained exclusively in simulation and transferred to the physical robot without real-world training or fine-tuning. These results show that physical context should be evaluated by the decisions it supports rather than by predictive accuracy alone. Code will be released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [337] [ForeseeDrive: Future-Token Conditioning for Anticipatory Generative Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14286)

Abstract: Generative driving planners can represent diverse ego futures, but a downstream scorer can choose only among sampled candidates; it cannot recover an absent maneuver. We present ForeseeDrive, which turns future scene evolution into a deployable interface for shaping candidate generation. Rather than reconstructing dense future states or scoring a fixed menu, ForeseeDrive derives query-indexed tokens from logged actor motion, occupancy evolution, route interactions, and TTC margins, and predicts the same token space from present context at deployment. The tokens condition trajectory geometry before candidates materialize. A zero-token-gated residual path and candidate-preservation loss limit unnecessary drift, while a TTC-margin guard targets brittle safe-to-unsafe boundary crossings. Under their respective benchmark protocols, ForeseeDrive reaches 89.2 PDMS on NAVSIM navtest, 0.41\,m average L2 and 0.05\% collision rate on nuScenes validation, and a 76.50 driving score on Bench2Drive. Controlled DiffusionDrive/NAVSIM diagnostics show an exact zero-token no-op, a small aligned-over-shuffled selected-PDM advantage under preservation, mixed oracle-best quality, and localized TTC-boundary failures. Together, the benchmark results and bounded mechanism tests support future-conditioned generation as a practical interface for anticipation. Code is included in the supplementary materials.

</details>

Abstract: Small, holonomic Autonomous Underwater Vehicles (AUVs) enable close-range inspection of underwater structures from arbitrary viewpoints, requiring accurate position and attitude tracking under state and input constraints. Model Predictive Contouring Control (MPCC) naturally handles these requirements, but relies on an accurate dynamics model, which is difficult to obtain underwater. In this paper, we present a nonlinear MPCC in all six Degrees of Freedom (DoF) for a holonomic AUV and alleviate the inaccuracies in the dynamics by augmenting the nominal model employed with residual dynamics learned via Gaussian Process (GP) regression. The learned residual is embedded in the optimal control problem through a zero-order robust optimization scheme, which keeps the controller real-time capable at 25 Hz. A simulation ablation shows that residual selection is essential when designing the controller, and simulation and in-water experiments with the Multimodality, Observing, Low-cost, Agile (MOLA) AUV validate the resulting controllers against a nominal MPCC and a field proven Proportional-Integral-Derivative (PID) controller.

</details>

Abstract: Despite the widespread adoption of autonomous vehicle technologies, reliable navigation within cluttered environments remains arduous due to the necessity of interaction-aware strategies; example scenarios include: narrow passages with multiple agents, lane changing in dense traffic, and crossing unsignalized intersections. These scenarios often require highly proactive strategies, simultaneously demanding interpretability and quick adaptability, which challenge end-to-end and data-driven methods. An effective strategy requires: scalability to multiple agents, adaptability to changes in other agents' behavior, and harmony with surrounding traffic without freezing or becoming overly conservative. To meet these requirements, we present SIGMO, a sigmoid-gated interaction-aware motion planner, with two main contributions. First, we formulate polynomial sigmoid functions that analytically estimate yield scores of surrounding agents using physics-grounded interaction features that capture driving intuitions. Second, we integrate the polynomial sigmoid functions into a receding-horizon planner, which facilitates interaction-aware planning with arbitrary trajectory predictions. We validate SIGMO in interaction-intensive simulations and hardware experiments. The results show that SIGMO balances proactive and defensive behavior while remaining computationally lightweight, serving as a strong baseline in a mobility stack. The simulation and hardware experiment videos are available here: https://sites.google.com/view/sigmoid-polygate.

</details>

Abstract: We describe our solution for the 2026 BEHAVIOR Challenge: 100 long-horizon household tasks in the OmniGibson simulator. We placed 9th on the final leaderboard, the only independent team in the top 10. The core of the solution is a single vision-language-action (VLA) model, one checkpoint for all 100 tasks, built on our 1st-place 2025 solution, which is based on $π_{0.5}$. On the 50 tasks shared with 2025, its Q rises from 0.260 to 0.371; across all 100 tasks it scores 0.392. Our main conclusion from 2025 was that the model was undertrained, so most of the 2026 work went into training the model for longer, optimizing some training parameters, making each training step slightly faster and reducing the gradient noise: a migration from 50 to 100 tasks (semantic stages built from the demonstrations' skill annotations, task and stage embeddings of new tasks warm-started from similar old ones, and a 21-dimensional action space matching the teleoperation controls); variance-reduced multi-sample flow matching with a simple noise-vs-compute model that picks the number of action-expert samples per observation without ablations; and a 200k-step training run, about one pass over the 211M frames of the demonstrations. A cloud evaluation infrastructure keeps a shared record with provenance, episode diagnostics and restorable simulator states, and runs six simulators on one GPU together faster than real time. On top of the learned policy, 14 tasks are routed to a GPT-6 Astra coding agent that controls the robot through observation and motion tools, starting every episode from a frozen copy of its own practice session on training instances. Across these 14 tasks, Astra's mean Q is 0.639, against 0.108 for the learned policy. The public submission included only part of the Astra results and scored 0.446 Q with 27.3% full-task success.

</details>

Abstract: Preventing human error in physical assembly tasks requires assistance systems that can intervene before mistakes are made. This is especially challenging in settings where users are free to choose the construction order and correctness depends on the evolving assembly rather than a fixed next step. We present TanCRAFT, a four-stage real-time predictive assistance system that tracks the scene state, reasons about the current partial assembly, forecasts the near-future motion of manipulated pieces, and intervenes when the predicted placement is incompatible with the target. We instantiate our framework and confirm its effectiveness in a controlled tangram assembly task through offline benchmarks and a within-subject user study. Our findings show that the predictive feedback significantly reduces mistakes relative to unguided and reactive sessions, while yielding the lowest mean perceived workload and shortest mean completion time. Overall, these results demonstrate the value of predictive feedback for error prevention and provide a step toward cost-aware assistance for automation and human-robot workcells.

</details>


### [342] [Is Your Robot's Memory Just a Clock? Temporal Shortcuts in Memory-Augmented VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14488)
*Chubin Zhang,Bo An*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Memory-augmented vision-language-action (VLA) policies are meant to remember what happened. Many of them, however, fill memory by time: they sample frames uniformly across the episode and stamp each frame with its time step. Because time passes even when nothing happens, such a memory may not tell waiting from working. We show that it does not. On the counting tasks of RoboMME, we make the robot briefly pause or fidget its wrist. Such a null event changes nothing the task depends on, yet a released memory VLA counts the interval as work done and stops early. We call this failure phantom progress. It cuts success from 47 to 16 of 50 on one task and from 44 to 10 on the other. When the same frames are withheld from memory, success returns, so the cause lies in memory rather than in the interruption itself. We trace the failure to a temporal shortcut: because demonstrations proceed at a steady pace, elapsed time predicts progress. Indeed, rescaling the time stamps alone makes the policy stop early when the clock runs fast and repeat when it runs slow. Surprisingly, removing the time encoding does not cure the failure, because the frames of a null event still occupy slots of the time-sampled memory. Across RoboMME's released designs, vulnerability follows how memory is indexed. Of the fixes the diagnosis suggests, training with injected pauses does not remove the failure and impairs counting, whereas indexing memory by events recovers half or more of the loss without retraining. A memory meant to remember what happened should count events rather than clock ticks.

</details>

Abstract: This paper explores a controller that is at the crossroads of neuromorphic, i.e., spike-based, and impulsive control systems. The controller consists of a single integrate-and-fire-like neuron that generates an input proportional to the system output when the neuron variable reaches a threshold. We prove that any static output-feedback stabilizable single-input single-output (SISO) linear time-invariant (LTI) plant can be made semi-globally asymptotically stable (sGAS) in closed loop with the proposed neuro-impulsive controller. This improves on existing fixed-amplitude neuromorphic controllers, which only achieve practical stability, but at the price of using graded spiking inputs. Moreover, we provide an algorithm for choosing appropriate controller parameters given a set of admissible initial conditions. A numerical example of an inverted pendulum linearized around its upright equilibrium illustrates the method.

</details>


### [344] [Opacity of Discrete Event Systems under Sensor Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13053)
*Shengbao Zheng,Yu Liu,Shaolong Shu,Feng Lin*

Abstract: Many information control problems in discrete event systems can be addressed using strong opacity, weak opacity, and non-opacity. These opacities depend on observations. If cyber attackers can attack sensors (or observation channels), then opacities will change. An opaque system may become not opaque, or a not opaque system may become opaque. Therefore, it is important to investigate opacities under sensor attacks. In this paper, we consider opacities under sensor attacks for two settings. The first one is the user has prior knowledge of sensor attacks in which the opacities are called as W-CA-opacities. The second one is the user has no prior knowledge of sensor attacks, and opacities are called as N-CA-opacities. For the former, we construct an augmented automaton that integrates the information of sensor attacks and then converts W-CA-opacities into classical opacities to solve the verification problem. For the latter, we construct the observers for the given system without sensor attacks and with sensor attacks, respectively. We then do the parallel operation on these two observers to obtain a method of verifying N-CA opacities. Furthermore, by adopting the detector technique, we find polynomial algorithms for verifying weak opacity and non opacity for both settings.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-agent systems increasingly operate in dynamic environments and execute complex tasks that require coordination, posing significant computational challenges. Counting tasks, which require a minimum number of agents to satisfy a given condition, arise naturally in such systems. Examples include surveillance tasks, where at least m drones must continuously monitor a critical area, and emergency-response tasks, where at least m robots must converge to an incident location. The identities of the agents assigned to these tasks may change dynamically depending on their states, input bounds, and individual objectives. In this work, we propose a control barrier function (CBF)-based framework for enforcing counting invariance and counting convergence tasks, without explicitly assigning agents or enumerating all possible subteams. We develop a recursion-based method for constructing differentiable CBFs for counting tasks. The resulting CBF constraints are incorporated into quadratic programs, enabling online controller synthesis. Numerical experiments demonstrate scalability to large multi-agent systems, while a multi-drone emergency-response case study illustrates the satisfaction of counting tasks.

</details>


### [346] [Fast Sampling or High Gain? iP versus PI Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13153)
*Mohamed Camil Belhadjoudja*

Main category: eess.SY

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Intelligent proportional (iP) controllers, introduced in the framework of model-free control, are often presented as an alternative to proportional-integral (PI) controllers that requires no model of the plant. This paper studies this property for a first-order linear plant under sampled-data implementation with a zero-order hold. When the time derivative of the output is estimated by a backward difference, the discrete-time iP and PI controllers are known to obey the same control law under a one-to-one correspondence between their parameters. Building on this correspondence, the paper derives necessary and sufficient conditions for exponential stability of the iP closed loop, in closed form, in terms of the controller parameters KP and alpha, the sampling period h, and the plant parameters. It then shows that, when the iP parameters are held fixed and the sampling period tends to zero, the closed-loop decay rate tends to the iP gain KP, provided that KP is positive and that the ratio rho between the input gain of the plant and alpha satisfies 0 < rho < 2. One closed-loop pole then behaves as 1 - h KP, while the other tends to 1 - rho. With fixed PI gains, by contrast, the decay rate tends to that of the continuous-time PI loop, which depends on the plant parameters. The two parametrizations therefore suggest different tuning procedures, although they achieve the same closed loops at every fixed sampling period. High gain for the PI is, in some sense, replaced by fast sampling of the iP. The results also make explicit which plant information the iP controller requires: the sign of the input gain and a bound on its magnitude in the limit of fast sampling, and bounds on both plant parameters at a given sampling period. Numerical experiments illustrate these results.

</details>

This paper develops a risk-aware DOE framework for battery arbitrage in unbalanced three-phase networks using a semidefinite program (SDP). Abstract: We propose a scheme for estimating the full states and parameters of a second-order Thévenin battery model, using distinct features of the measured current. An identification step, run during suitable rest intervals, identifies the resistances and capacitances of the two RC branches from the voltage relaxation by linear least squares, without knowledge of the open-circuit voltage (OCV) map. An online estimator then runs continuously: the polarization voltages are estimated by open-loop observers; the series resistance is updated at current jumps using least squares; the state of charge (SOC) is propagated by Coulomb counting using the current capacity estimate, and both estimates are updated periodically by a sliding-window regression. We show that the identification is exact under a mild rank condition, that the series resistance is recovered at the first current jump, and that the online state and capacity estimates converge exponentially under verifiable excitation conditions. We also establish robustness of the online estimator under bounded process disturbances and measurement noise.

</details>

IDC offers a direct end-to-end solution in the behavioral framework. Abstract: An impulsive model of endocrine regulation equipped with frequency- and amplitude-modulated pulsatile feedback is extended to study the dynamical effects pertaining to limitations due to the releasable hormone pool. The model describes a three-hormone regulatory loop and is applicable to endocrine systems such as the reproductive, stress, and growth hormone axes. Conditions guaranteeing the existence and uniqueness of a periodic solution with a single feedback-triggered impulse per period (a 1-cycle) are derived, together with monotonicity properties of its characteristics. An explicit Jacobian formula is obtained to assess local orbital stability. Model analysis demonstrates that a reduced hormone-pool replenishment rate produces a lower-amplitude, higher-frequency 1-cycle. This behavior is consistent with experimentally observed alterations in testosterone regulation in aging human males.

</details>

Abstract: We consider the problem of approximating backward reachable and reach-avoid tubes for high-dimensional nonlinear systems whose Hamiltonians require numerical optimization over constrained controls and disturbances. Existing neural Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) reachability methods alleviate the dimensionality bottleneck of grid-based solvers, but evaluating Hamiltonians without closed-form expressions introduces an additional computational challenge. We show that, under suitable assumptions, the gradients required for value-function training can be computed without differentiating through the inner optimization problem. Specifically, we establish an envelope-type gradient identity for the saddle point Hamiltonian, under which the optimal control and disturbance enter backpropagation as constants without gradient tracking. This enables end-to-end neural HJ reachability with numerically evaluated Hamiltonians while avoiding both solver unrolling and implicit differentiation. We demonstrate the accuracy and computational efficiency of the proposed approach on nonlinear reachability and reach-avoid problems of different scales.

</details>

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) offer a promising alternative to numerical integration for power system dynamic simulation; however, their conventional architectures can struggle to capture electromechanical oscillations. Although physics-informed Kolmogorov-Arnold networks (PIKANs) offer an alternative, their B-spline implementations incur substantial training costs. To address these limitations, this paper investigates Chebyshev PIKANs (c-PIKANs) and proposes a modal-augmented c-PIKAN (mac-PIKAN) based on a trial solution that incorporates the closed-form modal response of the linearized swing equation. First, the modal response is used to capture the dominant oscillatory dynamics. Then, a c-PIKAN learns a nonlinear correction weighted by a quadratic time-dependent factor, ensuring that the initial rotor angle and speed are satisfied exactly. Evaluations on the single-machine infinite-bus benchmark demonstrate the computational efficiency of c-PIKAN and show that mac-PIKAN reduces PINN rotor-angle and frequency errors from 1.59% to 0.29% and from 5.26% to 0.99%, respectively, while maintaining higher accuracy across training-data sizes and at lower damping levels.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural network (PINN) Kazantzis--Kravaris--Luenberger (KKL) observers require an approximate solution to a KKL partial differential equation (PDE) and an approximate inverse of this solution. Either approximation can leave a nonzero estimation bias even without measurement noise. In this paper, we remove both errors from the implemented observer by construction. We build an encoder containing an explicitly invertible neural map. Its closed-form inverse provides the internal state reconstruction used to evaluate the learned KKL residual and correct the latent dynamics. This construction makes the synchronization manifold exactly invariant for every value of the network weights. Based on contraction of the residual-corrected observer dynamics, we prove an input-to-state stability (ISS) result for the implemented error dynamics with respect to measurement noise. A direct consequence is that the estimation error converges exponentially to zero in the noise-free setting. We provide two numerical examples, the Van der Pol oscillator and the chaotic Lorenz system, in which the residual-corrected observers achieve exact noise-free convergence and improved error responses compared with released PINN--KKL baselines.

</details>


### [354] [Impact of Reduced-Order Modelling on Sum-of-Squares Region-of-Attraction Certification for Grid-Forming and Grid-Following Converters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13497)

Abstract: With the increasing penetration of power-electronic equipment, the transient stability of grid-forming (GFM) and grid-following (GFL) converters is increasingly characterized through the region of attraction (ROA) of the post-fault equilibrium. Sum-of-squares (SOS) programming yields certified inner approximations of the ROA, yet its computational cost grows rapidly with system order. Reduced-order models are therefore widely used to reduce computational cost and, more importantly, to enable analytical investigation. However, ROA analyses based on these models often assume that reproducing the converter's transient response, or retaining more dynamics in the correct time-frame, is sufficient to preserve the ROA of the full-order system. Using time-domain ODE simulations as a reference, the SOS-based ROA of each reduced-order GFM and GFL model is assessed against both its own simulated ROA and that of the corresponding full-order model, thereby separating SOS-induced conservativeness from model-order-induced ROA distortion. The results indicate that increasing reduced-order model complexity does not necessarily improve SOS-based ROA fidelity. In the cases examined, more complex reduced-order models yield lower SOS coverage of the full-order reference ROA, despite closer ODE-boundary agreement in some regions. Model selection should therefore consider both full-order ROA fidelity and certified coverage of the practical operating range, rather than model complexity alone.

</details>

Abstract: Dynamic wireless charging (DWC) has attracted increasing attention as a complement to conventional plug-in charging for electric vehicles (EVs). However, existing planning methods for dynamic wireless charging lanes (DWCLs) overlook realistic EV routing and charging behavior and lack explicit out-of-sample risk control under traffic-demand and renewable-energy uncertainties. To address these limitations, this paper proposes a data-driven two-stage robust planning framework that coordinates DWCL deployment with the operation of coupled traffic--power networks (CTPNs). A data-driven uncertainty set is constructed from joint historical scenarios to preserve spatio-temporal correlations without imposing parametric distributional assumptions. A modified user-equilibrium (MUE) model is proposed to capture realistic EV routing and charging behavior by incorporating state-of-charge (SoC) dynamics and endogenous charging decisions. The resulting two-stage robust model with mixed-integer recourse is reformulated as an equivalent single-stage scenario-approach problem and solved using a customized nested column-and-constraint generation algorithm. An \textit{a posteriori} feasibility guarantee is established to quantify out-of-sample risk. Based on this guarantee, the scenario set is iteratively enriched to meet a prescribed risk tolerance. Case studies on a 69-node power distribution network and a 24-node traffic network demonstrate that the proposed MUE model prevents overinvestment in DWCLs, while the proposed risk-quantification procedure enables a tunable profit--risk trade-off.

</details>

Abstract: Incremental input-to-state stability ($δ$-ISS) guarantees that the trajectories of a system converge towards one another, up to a gain on the difference of their inputs. Some existing learning-based designs that combine incremental stability with safety achieve only incremental input-to-state practical stability, whose non-vanishing offset can make the guarantee conservative. We consider control-affine systems with a known, full-row-rank input matrix and an unknown drift learned using Gaussian process regression. The prescribed safe set is a superlevel set of a smooth function satisfying a radial condition that ensures a diffeomorphism onto $\mathbb R^n$ and it need not contain any equilibrium of the uncontrolled system. Using this transformation, we design a smooth feedback law and derive sufficient conditions that, under the stated learning assumptions, guarantee with high probability both forward invariance of a compact subset of the safe set and $δ$-ISS on that subset. The incremental estimate uses the distance induced by the transformation and contains no practical offset. Simulations on a Van der Pol oscillator and a Lotka-Volterra system illustrate the proposed approach.

</details>

Abstract: Decaying DC (DDC) offsets severely compromise the accurate estimation of current phasors immediately following power grid fault occurrence. Consequently, conventional full-cycle discrete Fourier transform (FCDFT) algorithms experience decaying oscillatory magnitude errors, potentially causing distance relays to malfunction. This paper introduces a modulation-assisted technique designed to precisely eliminate the DDC components from fault current signals. By multiplying the fault signal with a high-frequency carrier and applying consecutive sliding Fourier projections, the proposed method effectively isolates the DDC signal while entirely rejecting higher-order harmonic interference. It utilizes a direct algebraic solution derived from a robust forward-difference operation across just three sequential projections to estimate and extract the DDC parameters. Furthermore, the algorithm seamlessly processes both single and multiple DDC components by dynamically tracking them as one equivalent first-order mode. Extensive evaluations are conducted to validate the proposed method accuracy. The proposed algorithm limits the maximum overshoot (MO) to 0.62% (0.59% for $k \geq 8$) for the evaluated DDC cases, and to 2.47% (0.95% at 20 dB for $k = 8$) under noisy conditions.

</details>

Abstract: This paper introduces a tensor-based framework for controller synthesis of stochastic multi-agent systems under temporal logic specifications that couple the agents, such as one agent reaching its goal only after another agent has reached its own. The agents are modeled as robust Markov decision processes (RMDPs), whose transition probabilities are only known to lie in an ambiguity set, for instance due to abstraction errors, learned models, or uncertain parameters. We show that the decoupled nature of the ambiguity sets allows one to decompose the computation of a lower bound on the satisfaction probabilities into per-agent factors, so that only the per-agent ambiguity sets need to be stored. This leads to improved memory complexity compared to monolithic approaches. We compare our approach with state-of-the-art approaches based on approximate simulation relations and with interval MDP abstractions. On a four-agent ordered reach-avoid benchmark, our approach certifies a lower bound twice as high as the simulation relation- based approach with 20 times less memory, and needs about 200 times less memory than the interval MDP abstraction, even at a resolution with about a million times more joint cells.

</details>

Abstract: We study how to unlock existing transmission capacity to accelerate large-load interconnection under differentiated quality-of-service (QoS) requirements. We establish an efficient framework to quantify firm and flexible hosting capacities and assess marginal values of grid upgrades. Specifically, we define multiple flexible capacity tiers with differentiated QoS limits and design a two-layer priority rule to allocate curtailment among tiers. We then formulate a mixed-integer chance-constrained model to jointly quantify firm and flexible capacities under QoS and priority constraints. To improve computational efficiency, we derive an equivalent aggregate formulation that eliminates individual curtailment variables and priority binaries without changing the feasible capacity set. Abstract: Multi-megawatt fixed-wing airborne wind energy systems (AWES) use an aircraft with large, slender wings that deform over the pumping cycle. To harvest maximum energy, the cycle is usually designed by means of dedicated reference trajectory optimization routines. It is well known that such routines rely heavily on the system model and that close alignment between the model and the physical system is required for the results to hold in practice. Nevertheless, the wing is usually treated as rigid and structural limits are respected indirectly through upper bounding of states and inputs. In this work, we directly enforce bounds on the elastic deflection and twist of the wing in the power-maximizing periodic optimal control problem. To that end, we model the wing by root-clamped modes, loaded by strip-theory aerodynamics and by the gravitational and inertial loads of the manoeuvring aircraft. To keep the problem tractable, the modal dynamics are replaced by their quasi-static equilibrium, which enters every discretization interval as algebraic constraints. For a multi-megawatt reference aircraft, the elastic twist near the wing tips reaches $1.03^\circ$ along the optimal rigid-wing trajectory. With a twist limit of $0.6^\circ$, the optimal trajectory delivers $31\,\%$ less mean power than the rigid-wing one, mainly through a lower tether tension during reel-out. Rigid-wing trajectory optimization can thus overestimate the power that the wing limits permit. Forward simulations that retain the modal dynamics demonstrate that the quasi-static deflection and twist deviate from the dynamic ones by less than $2\,\%$ of their limits. This work shows that elastic deformation can be accounted for in the trajectory optimization and that, depending on the wing limits, it can strongly reduce the achievable power.

</details>

Abstract: Rapid AI data-center power fluctuations challenge grid integration. Sustained grid-import smoothing requires allocating limited battery power and energy under uncertain demand. We formulate risk-aware BESS scheduling that coordinates advance reserve commitments and demand-responsive dispatch, accounting for operating costs, grid-ramp penalties, and their conditional value-at-risk. The trained layer is then frozen to guide demand-predictor learning through scheduling costs and ramping risk, enabling solver-free rolling-horizon scheduling. In a 10,000-minute simulation using loads derived from measured AI workloads, BS-DFL reduces mean absolute minute-to-minute grid-import changes by 71.70% and total scheduling cost by 18.01% relative to an MSE-trained predictor coupled with a mixed-integer linear programming solver, with an average decision time of 18.77 ms.

Abstract: This paper studies the safety verification problem for a general class of large-scale systems characterized by multiple safety indicator functions. Each safety indicator function satisfies a trajectory-based input-to-state safety (ISSf) estimate that provides a lower bound on its value along system trajectories in terms of the initial state, external inputs, and the other safety indicator functions, whose influence is quantified by continuous and nondecreasing gain functions. Within this framework, we establish a safety-oriented small-gain theorem and its corresponding corollaries that enable compositional and scalable safety verification of the interconnected system, provided that the interconnection gain functions satisfy a small-gain condition on the negative real axis. This condition concerns only the neighborhood of the safety boundary, thereby facilitating the application of our results to safety verification of multi-agent systems under distributed control. Our proof combines small-gain techniques with the lower-bound structure of ISSf estimates and develops an inductive comparison argument that establishes the ISSf property for the overall system throughout the maximal interval of existence. As an application, we establish robust safety for the natural distributed "half-spring" controller, theoretically supporting the computationally efficient and inspiring approach to safety-critical control of multi-agent systems in the presence of obstacles and external disturbances.

</details>

Abstract: Existing work on modeling destination-constrained dynamic systems has mainly focused on discrete-time formulations with linear destination constraints or Gaussian destination distributions. Since practical systems are more naturally modeled in continuous time and often subject to nonlinear/non-Gaussian constraints/distributions, this paper proposes a unified measure-transformation framework for continuous-time modeling of systems subject to deterministic or distributional constraints on a nonlinear function of the terminal state. The framework starts from an unconstrained Itô stochastic differential equation, typically derived from physical laws to describe the system's forward evolution without destination information, and takes its induced path measure as unconstrained measure. Doob's $h$-transform is employed to transform this measure into a constrained path measure incorporating destination information through an injected drift. The resulting system is proved to satisfy the constraints while preserving the laws of the unconstrained state process conditioned on the destination information. The transition density is derived, and the path measure of the distributionally constrained system is proved to uniquely minimize Kullback--Leibler divergence from the unconstrained path measure among those satisfying the same distributional constraint. Its injected drift is further shown to converge pointwise to that of the deterministically constrained system as the destination distribution shrinks to the prespecified value. For linear time-varying unconstrained systems, a simplified form of the constrained systems is derived in terms of the conditional expectation of the terminal state, leading to the development of trajectory-generation algorithms. Numerical simulations evaluate the proposed framework under both types of destination constraints.

</details>

Abstract: One of the major sources of inspiration for neuromorphic engineering is the central nervous system, which is orders of magnitude more efficient than classical digital clock-based computers. Neuromorphic control aims at recreating this efficiency in control systems, combined with (formal) stability and performance guarantees. In our previous work [1], we have established formal guarantees for the emulation of static output feedback controllers implemented using integrate-and-fire neurons. In this paper, we investigate the ``efficiency'' of the resulting controllers in terms of sparse spiking activity. By changing the network structure of our original implementation, foregoing the unit gain assumption made in our previous work, we will establish that the chosen architecture of the spiking controller significantly affects its sparsity and discuss the implications of these observations for the efficient implementation of spiking controllers.

We propose a hierarchical framework that optimises traffic signal timings, AV trajectories, and cyclist speed advice to reduce travel times for both motorised vehicles and cyclists. Abstract: This paper proposes a dynamic clustering framework for tube-based model predictive control (MPC) of coupled multi-agent systems. Agents within a cluster coordinate their dynamics explicitly, while couplings from agents outside the cluster are treated as bounded disturbances. For each admissible cluster, we construct a state-dependent mismatch measure from a robust upper bound on its predicted closed-loop cost and an optimistic, partition-independent lower bound. Combining this mismatch with a communication penalty enables online selection of the clustering topology, ranging from centralized to fully decentralized operation. The resulting switching controller is shown to preserve recursive feasibility and constraint satisfaction, and to render the closed-loop system practically asymptotically stable. Furthermore, the mismatch provides an upper bound on the finite-horizon performance difference with respect to centralized MPC. An eight-agent spring--damper example illustrates how the controller adapts cooperation while maintaining performance close to centralized MPC with reduced communication.

</details>

Abstract: Finite-time formation transitions are useful for coordinating multi-agent systems in time-critical missions, but convergence to a prescribed formation in finite time does not guarantee collision avoidance or communication connectivity during the transition. Existing approaches address finite-time coordination and safety-constrained formation control, yet explicit connections between disagreement during transitions, formation geometry, and coupling-gain selection need to be developed for certifying safe maneuvers. This paper develops a disagreement-based framework for certifying transient safety and connectivity during finite-time tracking of arbitrary prescribed formation trajectories. Local feedback guarantees exact finite-time tracking with a graph-independent settling-time bound, while a graph-dependent disagreement bound is used to derive sufficient geometric conditions for collision avoidance and connectivity preservation. These conditions further yield an offline coupling-gain selection rule based on the available geometric clearance. Numerical simulations illustrate the trade-off between convergence rate and control effort as the coupling gain increases. They also demonstrate the effectiveness and conservatism of the proposed certificates.

Abstract: Stochastic model predictive control uses disturbance statistics to set safety margins. Errors in these statistics can invalidate the safety guarantees. This paper therefore describes the uncertainty in the disturbance mean and covariance by a prescribed Gelbrich ambiguity set. This contains distributions of any shape whose moments are close in the Gelbrich distance to the nominal values, including distributions with unbounded support. For linear time-invariant systems with additive disturbances, we build on the indirect feedback paradigm, propagate this moment uncertainty along the error dynamics, and obtain a closed-form constraint tightening that is computed offline. Robustness enters as a single additive term that grows with the size of the ambiguity set, so the online problem remains a quadratic program. Recursive feasibility and closed-loop satisfaction of conditional value-at-risk (CVaR) constraints hold for every disturbance distribution in the set. We illustrate the method on a linearized planar quadrotor.

</details>

Abstract: With the rise of neuromorphic technologies, simple and tunable neuron models are needed to analyse and design spiking systems. However, the simplest neuron models, e.g., Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (LIF) models, usually fail to capture the excitable behaviours of biological neurons. This could be a significant limitation, as excitability enables reliable spike timing under parametric uncertainties. Recently, a minimal excitable model, the so-called High-Pass Model (HPM), was introduced in neuroengineering. It simply consists of a static sigmoid with a high-pass filter in positive feedback. Given its novelty, it is still unclear how easily the HPM can be tuned, and how much of the excitable behaviours of a biophysical neuron it can reproduce. To address these questions, we investigate the tunability of a first-order HPM sub- and supra-rheobase, i.e., below and above the onset of the tonic spiking regime (where the neuron spikes periodically), and compare its behaviours with those of a simple Hodgkin-Huxley-type Conductance-Based Model (CBM). Below rheobase, both models fire spikes in response to transient inputs, including post-inhibitory rebound spikes. Above rheobase, an affine reparametrization of the input of the HPM suffices to fit its frequency-current curve to that of a CBM. The two models mostly differ near their respective rheobases, where the first-order HPM lacks bistability and spike latency. We suggest that modifying the frequency-selective element of the HPM may be sufficient to overcome this limitation. These results highlight the potential of the HPM as a fundamental computational primitive in neuromorphic, spike-based technologies.

</details>

Abstract: Base stations account for a dominant share of energy consumption and operational carbon emissions in radio access networks. In O-RAN deployments with heterogeneous energy sources, however, minimizing energy consumption alone does not necessarily minimize carbon emissions. In this work, we investigate carbon-aware DRL for RU sleep control. Under the adopted source-matched operational accounting model, renewable-matched RUs are assigned zero direct operational emissions, whereas non-renewable-supplied RUs incur operational emissions. The problem is formulated as a Markov decision process and evaluated using two carbon-aware reward formulations and a conventional energy--QoS reward baseline. We implement and compare TD3, PPO, and a heuristic method under realistic mobility and 3GPP channel models across multiple renewable-matched RU layouts and traffic loads. Simulation results show that TD3 learns more energy-efficient activation policies than PPO, achieving approximately 3%--6% lower energy consumption in representative scenarios. TD3 also achieves 8%--12% lower operational emissions than PPO in scenarios with a low proportion of renewable-matched RUs and up to a 20% reduction relative to the heuristic baseline under moderate renewable availability and high traffic load. Moreover, the joint energy,carbon and QoS reward achieves approximately 12%--28% lower operational emissions than the energy and QoS reward, while maintaining comparable energy consumption.

</details>

Abstract: Recent advances in artificial deep learning-based power flow (PF) solvers have primarily focused on improving estimation accuracy, while their physical feasibility and operational reliability under realistic system variations remain insufficiently evaluated. This paper proposes a physics-informed evaluation framework for deep learning-based PF solvers under topology and load distribution shifts. Beyond conventional voltage-domain errors, the framework incorporates KCL gaps, branch-flow gaps, KVL residuals, continuous feasibility scores, and strict, practical, and relaxed PF feasibility rates. Four case studies are designed to investigate topology shifts, mixed topology training, load distribution shifts, and combined topology distribution shifts. Eight ANN architectures, including graph input models, load-only baselines, and physics-informed neural networks, are evaluated under consistent settings. The results demonstrate that low estimation errors do not necessarily correspond to physically feasible or operationally reliable PF solutions, particularly under combined system shifts. The proposed framework establishes an initial foundation and analysis for the development and verification of trustworthy deep learning-based PF solvers, providing support for their reliable deployment in future AI-enabled power system analysis.

</details>

Abstract: One approach to event-driven learning is to encode continuous signals as sparse events generated when signal changes exceed a prescribed threshold. However, this threshold is typically selected empirically, with limited theoretical guidance. This paper establishes a connection between threshold-based event encoding and discretized learning systems and identifies a dimensionless ratio governing information retention. From this connection, we derive an information-sufficiency boundary using normal-condition data and formulate a data-driven criterion that selects the sparsest threshold retaining a prescribed fraction of peak discriminative information. The framework is illustrated using event cameras and evaluated on power-system fault classification. Results show that the proposed design identifies sparse operating thresholds while exposing the tradeoff between information retention, event activity, and classification performance.

</details>

Abstract: This paper investigates probabilistic reachability for discrete-time linear stochastic systems subject to bounded multiplicative uncertainty and potentially unbounded additive noise. To address the conservatism of reachable sets constructed solely from covariance information, we develop two complementary approaches based on sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential matrix moment-generating-function characterizations. The sub-Gaussian approach relies on a worst-case envelope over the uncertainty support, whereas the sub-exponential approach exploits expectation-based propagation of the matrix variance proxy. Both approaches are used to construct probabilistic reachable sets with explicit probability guarantees, considering ellipsoidal confidence regions and half-space bounds. For the latter, we derive a necessary and sufficient condition under which the sub-exponential chance-constraint margin is no larger than its sub-Gaussian counterpart. Numerical results show that the proposed bounds can be less conservative than those based on the covariance, and illustrate the complementary nature of the two approaches across probability levels.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-vehicle trajectory planning for CAV requires balancing energy consumption and travel time while ensuring safety and scalability. While travel-time-optimal methods based on Integer Programming exhibit poor scalability in large-scale multi-vehicle scenarios, existing energy-oriented approaches fail to balance energy consumption and travel time. We extend a configuration-space framework for multi-vehicle trajectory planning to jointly locally optimize travel time and energy consumption. A weighted energy-travel time objective is combined with an efficient augmented DAG search on two-dimensional decompositions of a high-dimensional configuration space, enabling scalable and collision-free coordination of large numbers of vehicles. Although the proposed framework is applicable to other traffic scenarios, such as roundabouts and vehicle-following, we focus on intersections as a representative and challenging planning problem. To further improve performance for our methodology, we also extend the method with an insertion-ordering heuristic that exploits reduced-space geometric information. Combined with nonlinear model predictive control, the proposed framework is shown to scale to 40 vehicles, outperform MILP-based and convex optimization baselines in dense intersections, and reduce energy consumption at equalized travel times relative to time-optimal planning.

</details>

Abstract: The traditional Voltage Source Inverter (VSI) based Dual Active Bridge (DAB) utilizes a transformer at its center to provide galvanic isolation and a turns ratio for step up/down functionality. However, the transformer is often the limiting factor of the DAB, especially as semiconductor switching speeds improve, given steel core losses, winding losses, etc. This paper presents a new DAB architecture based on Current Source Inverters (CSIs) coupled through, and isolated by, an AC capacitive bridge, yielding a system called a Capacitive Bridge Transformer (CBT). The CBT architecture eliminates all magnetic components in the AC portion of the isolated DAB circuit, reducing frequency scaling barriers normally present in DABs. The paper introduces the CBT from the perspective of duality and then develops the DC-DC power flow equations, the internal apparent power equations, and effective turns ratio. The analytical models are verified by steady state and detailed time domain measurements on a 2 kW experimental prototype.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents a cascading contingency analysis and risk assessment framework that incorporates probabilistic mode transitions motivated by stochastic hybrid system (SHS) concepts together with power-flow-based stress evaluation. A two-stage modeling approach is adopted in which sensitivity-based analysis is first used to identify candidate cascading transitions, followed by DC power flow re-evaluation to accurately capture post-contingency stress redistribution, islanding behavior, and load shedding. A probabilistic propagation model is constructed using mode-dependent transition probabilities derived from line loading conditions. A Cascading Risk Index (CRI) is introduced to quantify the expected load shedding accumulated over cascade progression. Case studies on the IEEE 39-bus system demonstrate that the proposed framework captures diverse cascading routes under multiple initiating outages and identifies critical propagation patterns that are not revealed by N-1/N-2 screening. The results highlight the importance of SHS modeling in assessing cascading risk and providing a scalable framework for resilience evaluation in modern power systems.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we consider the problem of recompression of images from JPEG to JPEG AI format. We show that direct recompression from JPEG to JPEG~AI trained on uncompressed images is accompanied by the preservation of the JPEG image artifacts in the resulting JPEG~AI image. In order to avoid it, we evaluate an approach where an artifacts removal neural network is first applied to the input JPEG image as an external pre-filter. However, in this case, the processing time is increased by 29 times. Therefore, we propose an artifact-compensated training for JPEG AI and show that it is able to combine pre-filtering against target distortions with efficient compression without increase in the processing time.

</details>

Abstract: Ensembles of ImageNet-pretrained convolutional networks and Vision Transformers are a common way to improve multi-label chest X-ray classification. We ask whether such an ensemble is still worth its cost when a model pretrained on chest radiographs is available. On the official patient-disjoint split of NIH ChestX-ray14 (25,596 test images, 2,797 patients), we train five ImageNet-pretrained backbones and the EVA-X-S foundation model with validation-only model selection, compare five fusion rules, and estimate every difference with a patient-level paired bootstrap. EVA-X-S alone reached a test macro-AUROC of 0.828; the five-model ensemble reached 0.814 (difference -0.0136, 95% CI -0.0178 to -0.0098) at about 7.8 times the summed backbone latency and ten times the parameters. Averaging the five ImageNet models with EVA-X-S changed AUROC by -0.0056 (-0.0095 to -0.0021). With all five ImageNet backbones trained with the EVA-X-S recipe, their ensemble scored 0.817, still -0.0109 (-0.0142 to -0.0076) below EVA-X-S, and across three seeds EVA-X-S varied by at most 0.001 AUROC. Under the ImageNet models' recipe, however, EVA-X-S fell to 0.798. Our full-stacking configuration never outperformed calibrated averaging. In this setting, the tested ImageNet ensembles did not improve on a single chest X-ray foundation model fine-tuned with its own recipe.

</details>
