Abstract: Video applications are increasingly prevalent, yet their high data volume and temporal dependency make robust wireless transmission challenging under limited communication resources. To address this issue, we propose a token importance-aware semantic communication (Ti-SemCom) framework for bandwidth-efficient video transmission. Specifically, object and motion information is jointly extracted to identify communication-relevant regions, which are hierarchically associated with semantic tokens according to their importance. The transmitted tokens are then dynamically selected based on both token importance and the available bandwidth, prioritizing more informative video content under resource constraints. At the receiver, untransmitted regions reuse recently reconstructed references to further reduce redundant transmission. Experiments on the CAVIAR dataset show that Ti-SemCom can achieve an 88.3\% reduction in communication cost while maintaining competitive reconstruction quality.

</details>


### [381] [SNIF: a Self-Navigator Intelligent Filter correcting off-center and spin-history artifacts in quasi-random radial MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13056)
*Tanguy Boucneau,Brice Fernandez,Peder Larson,Luc Darrasse,Xavier Ma{î}tre*

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: For self-navigation and self-gating in MRI, self-navigators are extracted from the complex values of the k-space center, called DC-signal, through a temporal frequency analysis. However, this signal often carries artifacts. This paper introduces a Self-Navigator Intelligent Filter (SNIF) that models the physical origin of the k-space center artifacts to properly estimate them and thus more accurately remove them. The artifact affecting the DC-signal $S_{DC}$ generally has two main origins: the jitter of the k-space center (''off-center artifact'') and the cumulative history of the spins (''spin-history artifact''). Both add noise-like artifacts to the signal. The off-center effect can be modeled as a perturbative signal $S_0$ and the spin-history effect as a convolution kernel $h$ so that the measured DC-signal is $S_{DC} = S_m + S_0 \star h$, where $S_m$ is the motion-related component which effectively contains the expected self-navigators. Methods to compute $h$ and $S_0$ were developed, which gives the possibility to extract $S_m$ from $S_{DC}$. They were implemented and evaluated on thoracic UTE datasets acquired on a healthy volunteer. Qualitative visual comparison and quantitative spectral analysis of the DC-signal before and after SNIF demonstrate that the method improves both the accuracy and the precision of the extracted respiratory and cardiac self-navigators. By considering the physical origin of the DC-signal artifacts, SNIF makes both respiratory and cardiac self-navigators more sensitive to motion, which supports finer MRI motion tracking.

</details>

Abstract: AI agents can write code, use scientific software, and revise computational workflows, opening new possibilities for MRI research. Effective assistance requires connecting acquisition physics, data conventions, specialized tools, and scientific validation. We present MRFoundry, an open-source, extensible package that connects MRI knowledge to agent actions and output checks. Project-local memory preserves decisions and evidence, while reviewed contributions allow researchers to improve shared guidance. Researcher-supervised examples demonstrate skill-guided sequence auditing and acquired-data reconstruction, connecting tool use, output checks, and documented decisions. Alongside MRFoundry, we introduce MRI Agent Bench to compare agents with and without the package across models and MRI task domains. Across 240 paired Core evaluations spanning 24 task types and ten models, all-checks-passed completion increased from 67.5% without MRFoundry to 78.3% with it. Across 134 Hard pairs, completion increased from 36.6% to 44.8%. MRFoundry makes MRI workflow expertise available as reusable guidance that researchers can inspect, adapt, and extend within existing agent and scientific software environments. MRFoundry is openly available at https://github.com/KeWang0622/MRFoundry.

</details>

Abstract: Learned image compression (LIC) models achieve competitive rate-distortion performance with increasingly large networks, making network quantization important for efficient deployment. Directly quantizing LIC models remains difficult in post-training quantization (PTQ) because compression performance is highly sensitive to quantization noise. Existing methods typically focus on optimizing either the weights or the activations, resulting in substantial rate-distortion degradation. To address this limitation, we introduce a two-stage pipeline that uses SmoothQuant to balance activation and weight ranges, followed by GPTQ to adapt the transformed weights. The proposed joint search framework optimizes the balancing transformation and adapted weights under the rate-distortion objective, accounting for the coupling between the two stages. Experiments across multiple LIC architectures and activation quantization configurations show that the joint search strategy consistently reduces rate-distortion degradation after quantization.

</details>

Abstract: Intraoperative scene perception in ophthalmic microsurgery is limited by instrument occlusion: when a tool enters the high-magnification field, it conceals tissue from both the surgeon and the perception components of robot-assisted systems that process the video. Generative inpainting can remove the occluder but replaces the missing image content with synthesis, which is unsuitable when visual provenance matters. Many video inpainters also depend on future frames and cannot operate online. We present TransGHOST, a causal reference-guided reconstruction pipeline whose non-generative safe mode operates in real time. Every restored value is obtained by resampling a recorded frame using geometric transforms fitted only to visible regions. The system provides per-pixel confidence and provenance and marks unsupported pixels as abstentions. We evaluate TransGHOST on a new veterinary clinical corpus of canine cataract surgery with pixel-level instrument and anatomy labels. Only the occluded region is scored, and each scoring target is verified to differ from the reference supplied to the system. Safe mode reaches 31.89 dB masked PSNR and 0.915 masked SSIM at 51.7 ms median and 85.5 ms 95th-percentile latency. On a pre-declared held-out cohort using instrument cutouts excluded from segmentation training, it reaches 29.83 dB masked PSNR, while matched-mask evaluation on validation shows a 3.45 dB advantage over LaMa. Region-aware reference selection improves masked PSNR by 3.60 dB over recency when candidate references can themselves be occluded. Reference-age analysis further defines the operating interval for the brief, repeated occlusions targeted by TransGHOST.

</details>

Abstract: Text-guided segmentation models receive an image and a short caption about the lesion, and on public benchmarks the caption improves accuracy considerably. On colonoscopy and breast ultrasound, however, the size, count and location words of each caption were computed from the mask that later scores the prediction, and the chest X-ray captions were written by benchmark annotators. We tested what two published models take from them. On colonoscopy and chest X-ray, it is mostly the lesion's extent. Keeping only the size word or the lung-zone phrase recovers 84 to 98 percent of the improvement over an empty caption, and removing it loses 52 to 77 percent. The rest matters little, and a polyp called a rock is segmented just as well. We call this extent leakage. On breast ultrasound, nearly half the captions also name the diagnosis, which carries part of the benefit. Retraining on three datasets shows that models pick up this habit from their training captions, though only partly on breast ultrasound, and that it weakens their segmentation from the image alone. The leak can be measured without training a model, since caption words alone predict lesion area (coefficient of determination 0.54 to 0.77 on six of seven test sets). Without the test mask, the best method we tested, which estimates extent from the image, reaches 74.59 Dice on colonoscopy against the published 77.23. We propose an evaluation protocol that keeps what a caption says about the image apart from what it copies from the label.

</details>
